ICYMI: Call Of Duty: Warzone Is Now Available For Switch 2

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Much of the focus has been on Zelda: Ocarina of Time this week, but in case you missed it, Call of Duty: Warzone is now available to download for the Switch 2.

This is a free-to-play battle royale experience, where you can squad up and drop in. It's a separate mode from the Modern Warfare 4 single-player, multiplayer and DMZ mode.

It can be downloaded via the eShop and requires an Activision account to play. On our end, our total file size is now 122GB.

"The start of Season 1 marks the launch of Call of Duty: Warzone on Nintendo Switch™ 2! Drop into Hajin, Zodiac, and all the other maps and playlists as the free-to-play game arrives on the handheld system for the first time."

Alongside this is the release of Season 1 for Modern Warfare 4 and Warzone. In the MW4 multiplayer, this seasonal update comes loaded with additional multiplayer maps (Backstab, Canyon, Shoot House and more), modes, events, and rewards across both the game's multiplayer and DMZ. Dakota Johnson Does High-Low Dressing in a Hoodie and Tons of Diamonds

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Diamonds—and hoodies—really are a girl’s best friend. On Thursday night, Dakota Johnson offered up a dose of high-low dressing for a date night with Machine Gun Kelly.

Photographed outside of West Hollywood Italian hotspot Dan Tana’s, after attending a party hosted by Khaite, the Fifty Shades of Grey actor was seen in a monochrome little black look with subtle details. Her high-waisted miniskirt was trimmed with scalloped lace, her Gucci leather knee-high boots featured the house’s signature horsebit hardware, and her quilted shoulder bag from Valentino was studded with gold spikes.

To further accessorize, Johnson added six figures’ worth of jewelry, selecting a strand of tiger-eye beads from Laoli, set with four diamonds and an additional diamond pendant, and a 42-carat diamond necklace from Jessica McCormack.

Finally, to dress things down, she added a black zip-up sweatshirt to the ensemble. The cozy piece, with extra-long arms, a boxy silhouette, and a cropped hem, was embroidered with red lettering—identifying it as merch from Snoop Dogg’s production company, Death Row Pictures.

Next to her, MGK was seen wearing a deep-green satin bomber jacket over a yellow graphic hoodie, sweatpants, and fuzzy slippers—taking casual dressing to the next level. The two stars were first spotted together in September, after supposedly meeting at Taylor Swift and Travis Kelce’s July 3 wedding. While they have yet to confirm the relationship publicly, they have been frequently photographed together.

The rumored relationship followed Johnson’s breakup with artist Role Model. The actor, who previously dated Coldplay’s Chris Martin, joked about her tendency to date musicians while hosting Saturday Night Live—teasing that while most fans leave concerts with a T-shirt, she leaves them with long-term relationships.

Skip Ground Chuck: The Ideal Type Of Ground Beef For Tacos With Big Flavor

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

It goes without saying that meat forms the cornerstone of a ground beef taco. Yet while so many cooks focus on adding something (like cocoa powder) to ground beef for richer tacos, fewer pay attention to the cut at hand. After all, minced meat takes on not only various fat compositions, but can originate from different parts of the animal, each of which translates into a unique flavor and texture.

Especially common on grocery shelves is a product simply labeled ground beef, which means the meat mix consists of bits and pieces sourced from all around the cow. Also popular is ground chuck, a cut that turns out rich when ground, perfect for burgers and meatballs that will hold their shape during cooking. For tacos, though, reach for ground round instead; it's the ideal type for a flavorful pan-fried filling.

Round comes from a broad region near the back legs of a cow. It's an area high in connective tissue, but leaner than chuck. As a result, the ground beef doesn't turn too greasy when cooked, instead producing lean bites of meat that are amenable to the dish. The flavor is milder, too — making it an ideal canvas for your chosen seasonings. Put together, such characteristics yield a tasty beef character, but one that won't overwhelm your taco build.

Making the best ground beef taco meat is different from other recipes. Fat is flavor, and with the round's leaner composition than chuck, you're getting a less beefy experience. If you're looking to make juicy burger patties, that's a detractor. For ground beef tacos, though, such a restrained character is beneficial.

Aim for a ground round composition of around 85% lean and 15% fat beef. There's still enough fat to keep bites tender, but not an excess that'll have oil pooling in the pan. You'll let the intricate flavor of taco seasonings shine, but the beef still comes with its own delicate nuance. You can get unorthodox and add some of the best seasonings for beef, like pairing Guajillo chile powder with honey.

Furthermore, the ground round bits will cook down into a crumble, which lets you adhere spice onto a greater surface area. And once rolled into a tortilla or crispy taco shell, the beef is loose in tiny pieces, as is classic with the dish. Ground round strikes a perfect balance, making a taco rendition that hits the right notes without overwhelming the dish.

How to Winterize Salvias – 5 Easy Ways to Protect Plants and Guarantee Dazzling Blooms Next Year

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Beautiful plants and ideas to brighten your garden.

Expert tips for growing fruit, vegetables and herbs at home.

Plant These 7 Flower Seeds in November for Gorgeous Early Blooms Next Year

These 7 Plants Need Pruning Before Winter – November is Your Last Chance to Make the Cut

7 Foolproof Plants Anyone Can Propagate From Cuttings in November – Grow More of Your Favorite Shrubs for Free

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Many salvias can tough it out through fall, but even these easygoing plants need a helping hand in winter. Make time for our smart overwintering tips to protect these iconic favorites

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

As a seasoned gardener, if you ask me to name an easy-to-grow flowering perennial that returns reliably year after year, salvia would be at the top of my list. In my own garden, my ‘Hot Lips’ shrubby salvia shrugs off summer drought, asks for very little maintenance, and blooms generously from late spring through to November. Their nectar-rich flower spikes are magnets for hummingbirds, bees, and butterflies, drenching borders in rich purples, electric blues, hot pinks, and velvety crimsons.

Yet, despite their tough and easygoing nature, winter is the one season where salvias do need a bit of care and attention. The genus Salvia is vast, encompassing over a thousand species of annuals, woody sub-shrubs, and herbaceous perennials from regions as diverse as cold alpine meadows, dry Mediterranean hillsides, and tropical cloud forests. Because their natural habitats differ dramatically, their winter cold tolerance does, too. What protects a hardy meadow sage in New England will not save a tender Mexican bush salvia during a sudden polar snap. So winter salvia care needs to be adapted accordingly.

Taking action now, in this narrow window between autumn's close and winter's first freeze, is critical. Winter damage can happen fast, and yet knowing how to winterize salvias is surprisingly easy. By understanding your variety's cold tolerance and taking action fast, you can help prime your salvia in winter. Here’s how to easily guide your garden favorites through to next spring, so you can enjoy their lush, vibrant blooms for years to come.

The precise winter protection required will depend on where you live and the type of salvia you are growing. Some are cold-hardy perennials, while others are tender, frost-sensitive tropicals. So successful winterizing begins with identifying growth habit and hardiness classification. Deciduous herbaceous salvias like meadow sage (S. nemorosa cultivars like Caradonna and May Night) and woodland sage (S. pratensis) are cold-hardy in USDA hardiness zones 3-8. These perennials die back after hard frosts, resting safely underground before surging back in spring. They need little winter protection beyond good drainage and light insulation.

Shrubby, semi-hardy salvias (S. greggii and S. microphylla) occupy a middle ground, thriving in zones 6-9. These semi-evergreen sub-shrubs develop woody stems that can be damaged by sudden cold snaps or damp winter soils. In colder microclimates within these zones, they require protective mulching and shelter from wet winds. Finally, tender subtropical and tropical salvias, including velvet-flowered Amistad and Mexican bush sage (S. leucantha), flourish only in frost-free zones 8-11. These are large, bushy salvias that are killed by hard winter freezes, so in cooler zones, they require extra winter protection.

Before you begin, test your soil moisture levels and check that water drains away readily after rain. The XLUX Long Probe Deep Soil Moisture Meter, available from Amazon is a great dual soil moisture and pH diagnostic probe, giving instant readings to help you identify waterlogged soil pockets before the ground freezes. Once your soil is identified, you’re ready to take action. Here we clarify when to cut your salvias back, how to protect the roots from cold and wet conditions, and how to ensure you see your jewel-toned flowering favorites again next year.

When salvias start looking ragged, the natural impulse is to reach for the bypass pruners and shear everything back to a tidy clump. This could be the most damaging mistake you make. Many perennial salvias (especially semi-woody and herbaceous varieties) have hollow, straw-like stems. Pruning these down in late fall leaves those open tubes exposed to the elements. Rain and melting snow funnel down the stems and collect at the base, causing crown rot and fungal decay in freeze-thaw cycles.

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

It’s best to leave these old stems, faded flower spikes, and withered foliage in place to keep driving rain away from the vulnerable center. This dried top growth also traps air, forming an insulating buffer that protects the dormant buds huddled around the crown. So resist all heavy pruning until early spring, when fresh shoots begin pushing through the soil. Even if you live in a warm-winter area, it’s still best to leave major pruning until spring. The only fall cuts you should make are removing broken or diseased stems. Use sharp bypass pruners like the Felco 2 Bypass Pruning Shears, available from Amazon, when removing broken salvia stems.

Whenever I mulch plants for winter, I think of tucking a blanket around a sleeping child. Mulching salvias as cold weather is coming offers a layer of winter protection. When you apply mulch before the first hard freeze, you protect the root crown from severe cold and freeze-heave. In regions with fluctuating winter weather, bare soil expands and contracts, pushing shallow crowns up and exposing roots. Fully hardy salvia don’t need much winter protection, but a light mulch won’t hurt. When it comes to the semi-hardy salvias, however, mulching is a must to help overwintering plants.

The type of mulch, and how you apply it, are critical to avoid trapping moisture around the plant. Avoid dense, heavy materials like wet grass clippings, uncomposted manure, or thick leaf mold, which pack down and suffocate the plant. Instead, apply 3-4 inches (8-10cm) of loose, breathable mulch such as shredded pine bark, clean wheat straw, dry bracken, or horticultural grit. Keep it 2-3 inches (5-8cm) from the stems to avoid trapping moisture and inviting crown rot. Buy Bell Nursery Mini Straw Bales from The Home Depot for a loose yet insulating blanket.

Cold isn’t necessarily fatal for salvia plants in winter. But too much water will take out your salvias faster than a freeze. Native to sun-drenched Mediterranean climes, salvias demand sharp drainage. When sluggish drainage keeps soil saturated, oxygen is pushed out of the roots, leaving the crown vulnerable to fungal diseases. You’ll want to take steps to avoid poorly drained soil and winter wetness for any type of salvia.

If your garden has heavy clay soil, take action now before winter sets in. Gently scrape away decomposing leaves, as wet leaf mats retain moisture over the crown. Work a handful of coarse horticultural grit, sand or perlite into the soil surface around the plant to speed up surface runoff. Back to the Roots Organic Perlite from Walmart is a great option for improving soil aeration and drainage around salvias. For container-grown plants, elevate the pots off cold, wet patio slabs. Use terracotta pot feet, pot toes, or bricks to ensure drainage holes remain clear of standing water.

If your salvia are tender varieties like Amistad or S. leucantha, treat them like the cold vulnerable plants that they are. Sub-tropical salvias can’t tolerate hard frosts or frozen soil. Leaving them unprotected in northern yards guarantees root death. Fortunately, tender salvias overwinter indoors with minimal fuss, provided you bring them into shelter before the first hard frost blackens foliage. If your tender salvias are in pots, trim the longest branches back by half, then move to an unheated garage, frost-free shed, cold frame, or cool porch, and keep at 35-45°F (2-7°C).

For tender salvias in beds, carefully dig up the root ball with a spade, keeping plenty of soil around the roots. Place into a plastic nursery container, backfill loosely with dry potting mix, and move it undercover. Use plant caddies such as the Bloem Plant Caddy from The Home Depot to wheel larger container-based salvias into the garage with zero strain or back pain. The goal is to keep the plant dormant. Water sparingly: just a cup or two a month keeps the root ball from drying out.

Even with meticulous winter care, an unseasonal polar vortex or prolonged freeze can challenge borderline-hardy salvias. Taking a few semi-ripe salvia cuttings provides a terrific insurance policy. Select healthy, non-flowering or semi-ripe shoots (or snip the flower tip off a vigorous stem). Take a 3-4-inch (8-10cm) cutting below a leaf node. Strip the leaves from the bottom half, leaving 2-3 pairs of small leaves at the top. Dip into a rooting hormone such as HydroDynamics Clonex Rooting Gel from Amazon, to seal cut stems and trigger root growth. Insert the cuttings into small pots filled with a 50/50 mix of sterile seed compost and perlite.

Water gently, then place a clear plastic dome or bag over the pot to maintain humidity. Set your baby cuttings on a bright, warm windowsill, but out of direct sunlight. They should root within 3-4 weeks. Keep them lightly watered through winter, and pinch the tips in late winter to encourage bushy growth. Just bear in mind that rooting will be slower now than it would be with cuttings taken in late summer or early fall. Grown on a sunny indoor windowsill or greenhouse bench through winter, your salvia babies will hopefully be ready to plant out next May.

By the time spring arrives, you’ll be eager for warmer days and fresh growth (and so will your salvias!). As sunny afternoons begin warming the soil, check your overwintered salvias for vibrant green shoots emerging. That is your signal that winter danger has passed. You can then prune away old standing stems just above the fresh growth nodes. If you have woody salvias, spring is the time to shape the plant. Cut it back by a third to clean up the shrub. Spring is also the time to pull back protective mulches and reintroduce sheltered container plants to outdoor sunshine. With their crowns preserved through the cold months, your salvias will soon be rewarding you with months of lush and lively color.

Teo Spengler is a master gardener and a docent at the San Francisco Botanical Garden, where she hosts public tours. She has studied horticulture and written about nature, trees, plants and gardening for more than two decades. Before turning her focus to gardening, she worked as an attorney and legal writer, experience she also brings to her role as our Gardening Lawyer. Her extended family includes some 30 houseplants and hundreds of outdoor plants, including 250 trees, which are her main passion. Spengler currently splits her life between San Francisco and the French Basque Country, though she was raised in Alaska, giving her experience of gardening in a range of climates.

julia lohmann’s otherworldly kelp sculptures imagine a future beyond human-centered design

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

from leather-covered cow benches to towering kelp sculptures, julia lohmann questions how humans turn living organisms into design materials.

At London’s Design Museum, German-born designer Julia Lohmann arranged five towering kelp sculptures in a circle for the 2025 exhibition More than Human. Their translucent skins stretch across ribbed frameworks, resembling unfamiliar creatures brought up from the ocean floor. Titled Kelp Council, the installation imagines a gathering of marine organisms discussing the condition of their environment, with visitors invited to join the conversation.

As designers increasingly turn to seaweed as an alternative to conventional materials, Lohmann raises a question about the ecosystems supplying them. What happens when a natural resource becomes the focus of growing industrial demand? Through her sculptures and material experiments, she considers how design might work with nature while accounting for the living systems behind its materials.

Julia Lohmann’s interest in the origins of everyday materials began long before her experiments with seaweed. In 2004, she created Waltraud Cow-Bench, a leather-covered piece of furniture shaped to resemble a reclining cow. By returning the familiar material to the form of the animal it came from, she draws attention to a relationship that typically disappears during production.

This thinking eventually led her toward marine algae. During a 2013 residency at London’s Victoria and Albert Museum, she founded the Department of Seaweed, a collaborative research initiative exploring the properties of kelp and its potential applications in design. Working with scientists and other specialists, the designer began developing techniques that would allow the material’s natural characteristics to shape her sculptural forms.

In Kombu Ahtola (2020), pieces of seaweed are stretched between the ribs of a rattan framework. As the kelp dries, it contracts, pulling the structure into its curved shape. The material helps determine the sculpture’s final geometry, introducing a degree of unpredictability into the making process.

Her Hidaka Ohmu pavilion, also presented in 2020, brings these experiments to an architectural scale. Measuring approximately six meters across, the structure is covered in translucent seaweed panels that filter light into its curved interior. Visitors can move through the installation, experiencing the unusual texture and physical presence of a material more commonly associated with the ocean.

Kelp forests support complex marine ecosystems, providing habitats for numerous species. While seaweed offers promising possibilities for material development, expanding its cultivation and harvesting introduces ecological questions of its own. Lohmann’s research considers how these resources might be used while preserving the environments that sustain them.

Kelp Council brings this concern into a more speculative setting. By imagining marine organisms gathered around a discussion, Lohmann asks whose interests enter the decisions we make about natural resources. Her sculptures offer a physical expression of an idea that connects her early work with her ongoing seaweed research: that the future of sustainable design depends as much on how we obtain materials as on what we make from them.

JuliaLohmann, Oki Naganode, V&A London, 2013 | image courtesy the designerJulia Lohmann, Oki Naganode, MoMA, 2013 | image courtesy MoMA

My 9 Favorite Hotel Toiletry Brands, From Aesop To Le Labo, And More

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Luxury hotel brands try to differentiate themselves in all kinds of ways. One (arguably) minor way they do so is by partnering with other luxury brands on their toiletry and skincare lines. While I’m not suggesting that you should pick a hotel brand because of the toiletries that they offer, I think I’m not alone in preferring some brands to others.

In this post, I’d like to share my favorite hotel toiletry brands, and then I’m curious to hear what brands OMAAT readers prefer. To be clear, my rankings are based purely on my perception of products and brands, from the packaging, to the scent, to how it feels using them, to memories I associate with them. I’m not claiming any authority with my opinion, so please don’t ask me to defend my preferences!

Still, I’m sure I’m not alone in getting more excited about some toiletry brands than others. With that out of the way, below are some of my favorite hotel toiletry brands (let me also emphasize that not every single property belonging to these groups has the brand standard toiletries).

Le Labo generally supplies toiletries for EDITION and Park Hyatt hotels, as well as select Fairmont properties, and it’s probably my single favorite toiletry brand. I love how there are different scents, and how they’re often customized for individual hotels (with the branding on the bottles even reflecting that). I enjoy the scents, and also appreciate that they seem to be fairly mild.

Bamford toiletries are generally available at ultra-luxury hotel group Capella, and sister brand Patina. I absolutely love Bamford, and find the product scents to be pleasant, and I also like the simplicity of the branding. For airline geeks, Bamford is also nowadays the premium toiletry partner of Cathay Pacific, so don’t be surprised if you see these products in Cathay Pacific lounges, and onboard the carrier’s flights.

Within the past few years, Ritz-Carlton has started partnering with Diptyque on toiletries. What an absolutely massive upgrade this is, compared to the previous Asprey Purple Rain collection, which I personally found to be completely unremarkable. I find Diptyque to have a very pleasant scent without being overpowering. It’s something I otherwise associate with Qatar Airways, as it’s the only major airline to partner with the brand. Speaking of changes in recent years in the luxury Marriott portfolio, St. Regis has moved to SACHAJUAN, which I find to be unmemorable.

A few years ago, Waldorf Astoria made the switch from Salvatore Ferragamo toiletries to Aesop toiletries. I’d consider that to be a significant upgrade. I love the scent of Aesop, and it can’t help but always remind me of Cathay Pacific back in the day (which, as noted above, has now switched to Bamford).

I love BVLGARI toiletries, including the packaging, scent, and feeling of using them. The catch is that while many hotels have BVLGARI toiletries, there’s not much consistency as to which brands have them. Yes, as you’d expect, BVLGARI hotels have these products, but that’s a small hotel group, with under 10 properties. On top of that, you’ll find BVLGARI toiletries at select properties of Four Seasons, Rosewood, and more.

Acqua Di Parma is another one of my favorite hotel toiletry brands, in terms of packaging and scent. The catch is that there’s not a major hotel group that consistently stocks these products, though they’re common enough among hotel groups that I feel good about including it on the list. For example, while not brand standard, the St. Regis Venice uses Acqua Di Parma products.

Molton Brown makes great products (I particularly like the soap). While no major hotel group consistently has Molton Brown products, I find that they’re pretty common at luxury hotels, including at many Marriott Luxury Collection properties.

I think Byredo is worth mentioning, as you’ll find the Bal d’Afrique product line at many InterContinental properties, and the Le Chemin product line at many Luxury Collection properties. While I don’t like this quite as much as some of the brands mentioned above, I appreciate that it’s often available at more mid-range properties, where you won’t typically find brands like Aesop and Le Labo.

Admittedly this is a bit lower end than the other options, but I wanted to also throw in a toiletry brand from a more mid-range hotel brand. For upscale hotels, I’d say that MALIN+GOETZ, available at Le Meridien properties, is probably my favorite line.

The above are nine of my favorite hotel toiletry lines. As you can see, some are pretty consistently available at select hotel brands, while others are frequently offered, though not consistently at any brand. I don’t claim that my opinions are right, but the above are the toiletry brands I’m typically most excited to see at hotels.

What are your favorite hotel toiletry brands?

Most of these licensed product came from a sweatshop in China owned by a subsidiary of Hunter or Sysco. None of these are the real thing, real quality.

Cathay Pacific and the Park Hyatt Tokyo got me hooked on Aesop over a decade ago. It's my go-to brand at home. Very excited Macys.com now stocks Aesop as well, as I normally would get 1 pt/$ through their direct shopping portal, while Macys.com can have the occasional 10-12 pts/$. At $50+/bottle, that's a good chunk of Atmos points and status points!

Personally, a fan of Diptyque and Aesop, wherever it is.

I forget the name of the toiletry the Four Seasons Lanai at Manele Bay, but it's great. A more tropical scent, but not overpowering. My wife and I loved it.

I fell down a YouTube rabbit hole a few months ago, and some guy was narrating how his amenity bag had Diptyque-branded stuff. He pronounced it "DIP-tee-queue" (instead of "Dip-TEEK), and now whenever I see Diptyque, I smile and hear that pronunciation. Thank you everyone for allowing me to add literally nothing to the conversation. :)

Hyatt Guest Charged $50 For A “No-Show” Despite Staying—Points To The Bigger Risk Of Booking With Points

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

A Hyatt guest booked two nights with points, arrived in the middle of the night, and says the hotel charged an extra $50 for being a no-show — even though they showed up and stayed. Hyatt kept the points for both nights, too. After a month of calls, the guest still can’t get a straight answer. The customer service explanation they’ve received doesn’t match Hyatt’s award no show terms. But those terms are bad enough on their own: when you cancel an award past the deadline, or fail to arrive, you are supposed to get your points back and get stuck with a cash bill of an indeterminate amount instead. I’ve covered one of these charges for more than $11,000. The amount isn’t disclosed when you book. They Showed Up And Want Their $50 Back The guest describes a two-night points booking at a Hyatt in Palm Springs. They arrived in the wee hours after the scheduled arrival date. They’re happy to pay the points for both nights that they reserved and stayed. They’re disputing the additional $50: Yeah crazy right! I am not trying to get my night refunded at all, just the no show fee. In the customer service conversation the guest shared, Hyatt’s agent says the first night’s points were forfeited because they didn’t show up, and that a cash no show fee still applies. The agent compares this to checking in and then leaving early, when unused points wouldn’t be returned. The guest says repeated calls to the hotel and Hyatt have ended with being placed on hold and disconnected. The chat ends with a case number and a promise of an email within 72 hours. Is This Right? from r/hyatt If you’re arriving in the middle of the night, tell the hotel and have them note that you’re still coming. Otherwise their overnight audit can mark you as a no show. Here it seems like the reservation was marked as a no show and then reinstated when the guest arrived, without the billing getting properly reconciled. Hyatt’s Agent Appears To Be Mixing Up Two Different Rules Under Hyatt’s award terms, a late cancellation or no-show generally means a cash penalty under the property’s policy, with the points or award returned. The rate is the hotel’s Standard Rate being offered when the charge is made. If Hyatt can’t collect payment, it retains or deducts the points instead. Guests need to find a way to make the cash uncollectible! That’s different from the rule for someone who checks in and departs early. Hyatt’s terms do provide for forfeiting unused award nights in that situation. The agent appears to think they’re applying that separate rule to a supposed no show. If the hotel honored both nights as an award stay, using both nights’ points makes sense. There’s no coherent explanation for the $50, and it’s clearly not the room rate penalty for a no show or late cancel which would be much higher. It might even be an internal award reimbursement amount incorrectly charged to the guest. You Book With Points But The Cancellation Penalty Is (A Lot Of) Cash Most people reasonably assume that if they book a nonrefundable award—or cancel after the deadline they’ll lose the points they used. That’s what they agreed to spend. Instead, major hotel programs return the points and let the hotel charge their credit card instead: Marriott: Section 3.2.d provides for the property’s applicable cash cancellation fee and redeposit of the points. Hilton: terms expressly permit cash penalties on reward stays. The hotel can specify its fee; otherwise the fallback is one night’s room and tax at its Best Available Rate for that date. IHG: terms authorize a cash no-show charge under the hotel’s policy. Six Senses is an exception: its award rule provides for forfeiting the points or free night certificate instead. A Hilton guest reported getting 20,000 points back and being charged £99 after arranging a morning arrival and actually using the room. And there was a Holiday Inn placing a €103.69 hold on an award guest’s card specifically to secure a potential no-show charge. That guest stayed and the hold was released. You’re Never Told The Amount You’re On The Hook For I once booked a Hyatt stay for 90,000 points. The confirmation said the cancellation penalty was “50 PCT OF STAY.” I’d read that as 45,000 points. Instead, I was told I’d owe half the paid rate. My concierge ultimately got it waived. I had Covid and said that if they were going to charge for the late cancel, I’d be forced to show up. And while I was in the lobby I’d have to be honest with other guests that the hotel pressured me to come and expose them. Specifying that the penalty is one night doesn’t put guests on notice for what Hyatt actually intends to charge – and their rules just reference the rate when the charge is made, whatever that may be. I’ve also received a Marriott hotel’s warning of a $1,999-plus-tax penalty on an award reservation. At least that property told me the amount. It did so after I’d booked. I don’t see how a guest meaningfully agrees to thousands of dollars in liability when the booking never tells them the amount. These booking disclosures seem ripe for a class action. Some of the examples I’ve covered: $11,212.50 at Montaneros in Vail: The guest cancelled 58 days before arrival, just inside a 60-day deadline. Hyatt ultimately confirmed a full refund and said it was examining the cancellation language for Destination Residences. $5,500 after a death in the family: A guest had to cancel a 63,000-point Lake Como stay because his father’s funeral conflicted with the trip. $3,690 at Thompson Central Park: A 58,000-point booking was noncancellable from the outset. The guest cancelled months ahead, got the points back, and then received the cash invoice three weeks later. A flight cancellation can trigger this just as easily as a change of plans. You may be perfectly willing to lose the points for the night you missed. That doesn’t mean you budgeted for buying the room all over again at a cash price you weren’t quoted. Starwood Had A Better Way Of Handling This The underlying problem is that the hotel and loyalty program are separate parties. The hotel doesn’t keep your points as payment. The loyalty program does, and it pays cash to the hotel. It doesn’t want to pay for an award stay that never happened, because that’s ripe for fraud – the hotel redeems a bunch of no show stays on nights they’re near full occupancy to scam the program for increased reimbursement rates. As I explained about Starwood years ago, reimbursing unused award reservations could let hotels collect for stays that weren’t real. High occupancy can make award reimbursements much more valuable, creating an incentive to manipulate the numbers. Starwood’s own representative acknowledged the need to verify stays to prevent fraud, while distinguishing that from the cancellation penalty imposed on members. And the Parker Meridien litigation was about inflated occupancy to extract higher award reimbursements. But making customers pay an undisclosed retail rate isn’t the only way to control reimbursement. It can even leave the hotel better much off when a guest misses the stay than when they actually arrive because the cash penalty will generally exceed what the program would have paid by a lot. At the Andaz Papagayo, I helped get a $7,000 penalty reversed after a cancellation six months ahead. The property had all that time to sell the room again. Starwood eventually allowed members to substitute points forfeiture for the cash charge. You could request the cash refund within 60 days of the scheduled arrival date and give up the points instead. That gave the member a choice, rather than simply letting the hotel claim a cash windfall. Marriott didn’t preserve that protection when they combined programs, but Hyatt could adopt it. Hyatt’s Customer Service Should Be Able To Untangle A $50 Bill In June 2025, I reported Hyatt’s major U.S. customer service cuts. The company confirmed a reduction of about 30% across its Americas guest services and support teams. Nebraska’s layoff listing records 286 affected jobs associated with Omaha and remote work. I’ve noticed a decline in central customer service, especially through Twitter direct messages. My experience with that team immediately after the cuts was still good. More recently, it hasn’t been as good. Here, instead of sorting out whether both award nights were honored, what happened to the no show transaction, and what the $50 actually was for, the agent offers an explanation that mixes together different rules and sends the guest back to the hotel. If the hotel used both nights as an award stay, I’d expect the points to remain deducted and the unexplained $50 to come off. If it insists the first night was a no show, Hyatt needs to reconcile that with its points return rule (and the fact that the guest was literally there and stayed in the room). A member shouldn’t need a month of calls to get someone to sort this. Topics on this pageHotelHyattLoyalty programAmericasAndaz PapagayoHiltonHoliday InnIHG Hotels & ResortsMarriott InternationalNebraskaOmahaPalm SpringsStarwood HotelsThompson Central Park New York+11 more More From View from the Wing

Starwood eventually allowed members to substitute points forfeiture for the cash charge. You could request the cash refund within 60 days of the scheduled arrival date and give up the points instead. That gave the member a choice, rather than simply letting the hotel claim a cash windfall. Marriott didn’t preserve that protection when they combined programs, but Hyatt could adopt it. Hyatt’s Customer Service Should Be Able To Untangle A $50 Bill In June 2025, I reported Hyatt’s major U.S. customer service cuts. The company confirmed a reduction of about 30% across its Americas guest services and support teams. Nebraska’s layoff listing records 286 affected jobs associated with Omaha and remote work. I’ve noticed a decline in central customer service, especially through Twitter direct messages. My experience with that team immediately after the cuts was still good. More recently, it hasn’t been as good. Here, instead of sorting out whether both award nights were honored, what happened to the no show transaction, and what the $50 actually was for, the agent offers an explanation that mixes together different rules and sends the guest back to the hotel. If the hotel used both nights as an award stay, I’d expect the points to remain deducted and the unexplained $50 to come off. If it insists the first night was a no show, Hyatt needs to reconcile that with its points return rule (and the fact that the guest was literally there and stayed in the room). A member shouldn’t need a month of calls to get someone to sort this. Topics on this pageHotelHyattLoyalty programAmericasAndaz PapagayoHiltonHoliday InnIHG Hotels & ResortsMarriott InternationalNebraskaOmahaPalm SpringsStarwood HotelsThompson Central Park New York+11 more More From View from the Wing

Most people reasonably assume that if they book a nonrefundable award—or cancel after the deadline they’ll lose the points they used. That’s what they agreed to spend. Instead, major hotel programs return the points and let the hotel charge their credit card instead:

A Hilton guest reported getting 20,000 points back and being charged £99 after arranging a morning arrival and actually using the room. And there was a Holiday Inn placing a €103.69 hold on an award guest’s card specifically to secure a potential no-show charge. That guest stayed and the hold was released.

I once booked a Hyatt stay for 90,000 points. The confirmation said the cancellation penalty was “50 PCT OF STAY.” I’d read that as 45,000 points. Instead, I was told I’d owe half the paid rate. My concierge ultimately got it waived. I had Covid and said that if they were going to charge for the late cancel, I’d be forced to show up. And while I was in the lobby I’d have to be honest with other guests that the hotel pressured me to come and expose them. Specifying that the penalty is one night doesn’t put guests on notice for what Hyatt actually intends to charge – and their rules just reference the rate when the charge is made, whatever that may be. I’ve also received a Marriott hotel’s warning of a $1,999-plus-tax penalty on an award reservation. At least that property told me the amount. It did so after I’d booked. I don’t see how a guest meaningfully agrees to thousands of dollars in liability when the booking never tells them the amount. These booking disclosures seem ripe for a class action. Some of the examples I’ve covered: $11,212.50 at Montaneros in Vail: The guest cancelled 58 days before arrival, just inside a 60-day deadline. Hyatt ultimately confirmed a full refund and said it was examining the cancellation language for Destination Residences. $5,500 after a death in the family: A guest had to cancel a 63,000-point Lake Como stay because his father’s funeral conflicted with the trip. $3,690 at Thompson Central Park: A 58,000-point booking was noncancellable from the outset. The guest cancelled months ahead, got the points back, and then received the cash invoice three weeks later. A flight cancellation can trigger this just as easily as a change of plans. You may be perfectly willing to lose the points for the night you missed. That doesn’t mean you budgeted for buying the room all over again at a cash price you weren’t quoted. Starwood Had A Better Way Of Handling This The underlying problem is that the hotel and loyalty program are separate parties. The hotel doesn’t keep your points as payment. The loyalty program does, and it pays cash to the hotel. It doesn’t want to pay for an award stay that never happened, because that’s ripe for fraud – the hotel redeems a bunch of no show stays on nights they’re near full occupancy to scam the program for increased reimbursement rates. As I explained about Starwood years ago, reimbursing unused award reservations could let hotels collect for stays that weren’t real. High occupancy can make award reimbursements much more valuable, creating an incentive to manipulate the numbers. Starwood’s own representative acknowledged the need to verify stays to prevent fraud, while distinguishing that from the cancellation penalty imposed on members. And the Parker Meridien litigation was about inflated occupancy to extract higher award reimbursements. But making customers pay an undisclosed retail rate isn’t the only way to control reimbursement. It can even leave the hotel better much off when a guest misses the stay than when they actually arrive because the cash penalty will generally exceed what the program would have paid by a lot. At the Andaz Papagayo, I helped get a $7,000 penalty reversed after a cancellation six months ahead. The property had all that time to sell the room again. Starwood eventually allowed members to substitute points forfeiture for the cash charge. You could request the cash refund within 60 days of the scheduled arrival date and give up the points instead. That gave the member a choice, rather than simply letting the hotel claim a cash windfall. Marriott didn’t preserve that protection when they combined programs, but Hyatt could adopt it. The company confirmed a reduction of about 30% across its Americas guest services and support teams. Nebraska’s layoff listing records 286 affected jobs associated with Omaha and remote work. I’ve noticed a decline in central customer service, especially through Twitter direct messages. My experience with that team immediately after the cuts was still good. More recently, it hasn’t been as good. Samyang AF 60-180mm f/2.8 Review: A Trinity Completed

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Samyang AF 60-180mm f/2.8 Review: A Trinity Completed

The two major third-party manufacturers from Japan have a complete trinity of full-frame professional f/2.8 zoom lenses. Sigma has long had a complete set, and Tamron has its excellent G2 series finalized. The Chinese companies are only now venturing into the autofocusing pro zoom lens market, but Samyang, out of Korea, has worked very hard — and quickly — to complete its full set, too. With the release of the new $1,099 Samyang X Schneider Kreuznach AF 60-180mm f/2.8, that goal is realized.This lens has some surprises up its sleeve, but the experience still feels lacking in features and refinement. PetaPixel Score 7/10 ProsVery sharp right to the cornersLightweight and compactBokeh has a pleasing rendering of backgroundsConsButtons and controls feel down-marketBasic and slower stepping autofocus motorNo optical stabilizationNo aperture ring This lens is being released for E-mount and L-mount cameras, and whether you should buy it will be highly dependent on that lens mount.

This lens has some surprises up its sleeve, but the experience still feels lacking in features and refinement.

I’m going to get this out of the way first: Samyang lenses, also sold under the Rokinon brand in North America, have always aimed at a budget price point and, as a result, rely heavily on plastic construction. Okay, lots of companies do, but Samyang lenses always just look and feel a little less refined and a little more basic than some of the others.

I’m not going to go so far as to say that this negatively impacts the build quality. I know many Rokinon users who have had years of reliable service out of their lenses. But the buttons and controls always feel a little flimsy and make obtrusive clicking noises when pressed. The plastic hood is basic and twists into place with a little bit of coercion.

The rubber rings feature two different textures, which Samyang usually likes to use. There is a custom button and an AF/MF switch, along with an unexpected locking switch for the zoom mechanism. The manual focus ring has a nice smoothness to it, but the zoom ring takes a bit of force to get moving and lacks refinement.

Flicking this will keep the zoom at the 60mm mark, but you do have to unlock it before zooming again. You will not find an aperture ring on this lens, but this is pretty standard at this price point. Samyang lenses do have some baffles and a little weather sealing incorporated into the lens mount.

With the 77mm filter threads on the front, this lens looks fairly hefty, but with an overall weight of 730 grams, it is actually lighter than the Tamron and Sigma alternatives. I also like that the lens doesn’t extend that far when zoomed to 180mm, and the point of balance doesn’t change much either. However, part of the lighter weight comes with a lack of optical image stabilization within the lens.

I was worried about the bokeh on this lens when I started with specular highlight tests. At the widest apertures, the highlights have a truncated look, which I do not prefer, and there are some slight onion rings present as well. However, at tighter apertures, the highlights are fairly round, and more importantly, the actual rendering of the out-of-focus areas of the images is quite smooth. I expected a harsher edge to the bokeh with a more distracting rendering, but the backgrounds looked soft and pleasant. Other than the distracting highlights, this lens presents shallow depth of field beautifully.

There is a little bit of flare present, which is something I found with the other Samyang f/2.8 lenses. At f/2.8, there is some green ghosting and a bit of veiled flare washing out the image. The green cast stays at tighter apertures but doesn’t get too obnoxious. Overall, the flare control is decent, and the hood will do a good job of blocking extraneous light. Speaking of color casts, expect a little bit of LoCA at the wider apertures. These longitudinal chromatic aberrations will present with a slight green and magenta split, but the effect is minimal and usually won’t cause much concern in the vast majority of your images.

Sharpness is where this lens excels, with excellent detail in the center of the image right from the start. I found the 180mm mark to be particularly good in the center at f/2.8, with slight improvement to be found at tighter apertures. The corners at the long end were also impressive, with more detail than I expected at f/2.8 and fairly low vignetting. Closing the aperture will improve things as usual, but I was impressed by how good the corner detail was overall. At the 60mm mark, the story is similar. The center sharpness is maybe slightly softer at this focal length, but still very acceptable. The corners were also similarly excellent, with far better-than-expected detail. This Samyang lens has the most consistent corners and overall detail compared to its third-party contemporaries.

The lack of lens-based image stabilization is unfortunate, although to be fair, the impact will largely depend on what camera body you are using. Clearly, the lack of a stabilizer keeps the cost down a bit and shaves off some weight. Another cost-cutting choice is the use of a more basic stepping motor for focus. Focusing is smooth and accurate, but the speed is noticeably slower than the competition. If you want to tackle faster sports and action, you might want to consider alternatives.

Kudos to Samyang for finalizing a trilogy of professional-grade f/2.8 full-frame zoom lenses. However, should you buy this set or even any of these lenses individually? Here is what we can say for certain: in my opinion, Samyang lenses are well-built but lack refinement, and tend to lack extra controls and aperture rings.

They always feel a little down-market in the hand to me, but the optical performance is formidable. This 60-180mm f/2.8 is both compact and frighteningly sharp. Its optical performance beyond that is certainly acceptable, but it doesn’t ever seem to shine in any other regard. Samyang has a 14-24mm and 24-60mm lens, both with f/2.8 apertures, and they are both capable of capturing great images. However, in a field of fierce competition, Samyang lenses may struggle to find a foothold.

Samyang AF 14-24mm f/2.8Buy new on B&HSamyang AF 14-24mm f/2.8Buy used on KEH.comSamyang AF 24-60mm f/2.8Buy new on B&HSamyang AF 24-60mm f/2.8Buy used on KEH.com

In Sony E-mount, this 60-180mm lens goes up against the Tamron 70-180mm f/2.8 Di III VC VXD G2, which almost exactly matches the $1,099 Samyang.

In L-mount, it only has to compete with the Sigma 70-200mm f/2.8 Sports and Panasonic S 70-200mm f/2.8, both of which are much larger and more expensive options. I’d probably go for the Tamron 70-180mm f/2.8 myself due to the faster AF experience and optical stabilization. However, if you value maximum sharpness and slightly less weight, and have a camera body with IBIS, or if you are looking for an excellent option in L-mount, the Samyang 60-180mm could be a sound choice.

Sigma 70-200mm f/2.8 SportsBuy new on B&HSigma 70-200mm f/2.8 SportsBuy used on KEH.comPanasonic Lumix S 70-200mm f/2.8Buy new on B&HPanasonic Lumix S 70-200mm f/2.8Buy used on KEH.com

Regardless of lens mount, the Sigma 70-200mm f/2.8 Sports is going to be much larger at a higher cost, and only makes sense if you want to use teleconverters. This is pretty much the same story for the Panasonic S 70-200mm f/2.8 as well. The Tamron does offer similar optical performance with better AF and stabilization, and is even more compact and manageable. The Samyang 60-180mm should put pressure on Sigma to make a better alternative, but Tamron has nothing to worry about.

Yes. This lens is still going to deliver excellent images at a good price and in a compact chassis. Whichever way you slice it, the Samyang is still a good tool for the dollar.

Dunkin' Is Making 3 Childhood Cereal Favorites A Little More Grown-Up

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

I didn't think my childhood breakfast needed a makeover, but I'm willing to hear them out.

I'm not that convinced Cinnamon Toast Crunch, Reese's Puffs, or Golden Grahams need much improving. All three have been breakfast staples for decades, and based on how long they’ve been on store shelves, it’s clear they've done pretty well for themselves.

But if we're going to mess with the cereals we grew up eating, borrowing a few flavors from Dunkin' isn't the worst idea.

The chain is teaming up with General Mills on three limited-edition varieties arriving this December, each taking its own approach to the coffee-influenced variation.

The new Peanut Butter Mocha flavor sounds like a pretty natural fit, considering chocolate and peanut butter are already involved. Meanwhile, Cinnamon Toast Crunch Cinnamon Caramel Latte adds a little caramel to its familiar cinnamon flavor, while Golden Grahams Vanilla Graham Latte gives those honey-graham squares a vanilla coffee-shop makeover.

And before any parents start worrying about their kids bouncing off the walls after breakfast, there's no actual coffee or caffeine in these boxes. The flavors are inspired by Dunkin's drinks, so unfortunately, you'll still need the real thing if you're looking for a morning pick-me-up.

Brian Gilbert, vice president of retail business development at Inspire Brands, said in a statement that the collaboration allows Dunkin' to “bring the flavors of some fan-favorite Dunkin’ beverages beyond the cup and into the cereal bowl.”

All three are scheduled to arrive in December, although you'll have to visit Walmart specifically for the Golden Grahams version, while the other two will be available at retailers nationwide.

And judging by early reactions online, plenty of people are just as interested as I am in giving them a shot.

"Those Golden Grahams finna slap 👋🏽" one person predicted, while another wrote, "I'm definitely going try all of them 💯". General Mills' previous cereal experiments seem to have earned it some goodwill, too. Its Reese's Puffs PB&J variety got a shoutout from one fan: "The pb&j Reese's cereal is really good. Excited for these!"

And someone else decided to get to the point and address Dunkin' directly, writing, "let me go head and send you my address NOW!!!"

As someone who loves the taste of coffee, I have a feeling these are going to be right up my alley. My morning iced coffee, though? That's never getting replaced.

Lamborghini Gallardo’s Original Designer Polishes His Masterpiece

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

The Cavën Atelier Hommage 26 adds modern tech, swaths of carbon fiber, and more power to Italdesign's beloved baby Lambo.

Before it threw in the towel and decided to get in the luxury crossover game with the Urus, the Gallardo was Lamborghini's bestselling model. Careful input from parent company, Audi, meant a rip-roaring V-10 and all-wheel-drive grip, and Italian design house Italdesign clothed this in angular styling that merged supercar and stealth fighter into a great-looking machine. It wasn't the top-dog Lamborghini at the time, but it packed a punch.

The initial work at Italdesign was done by none other than Fabrizio Giugiaro, son of the legendary Giorgetto. The Gallardo would later be polished by Luc Donckerwolke, but it seems like Fabrizio had a few finishing touches of his own to add. Thanks to Cavën Atelier, a Spanish engineering firm, Giugiaro Jr. has had a crack at reworking his 2003 original with modern tech and plenty of tweaks.

See all results for used Lamborghini for sale near 78745

The Hommage 26 starts with a donor Gallardo, similar to the Encor Series 1 Lotus Esprit restomod, which is then thoroughly reworked with carbon-fiber bodywork throughout. The front end resembles the original, but now incorporates modern lighting and pop-down headlights.

Like an old silhouette Trans-Am Audi, only the top sliver of the car is unchanged, with the same roofline and glass. Everything else is subtly reshaped, including a new rear fascia with massive rectangular exhaust pipes and integrated rear active aerodynamic panels like those you might find in a Pagani Huayra.

Under this new skin, the car's 5.0-liter V-10 now spins to 8500 rpm, producing either 533 hp in GT trim or as much as 552 in full-fat Performance spec. There’s active suspension, upgraded brakes, and the Hommage 26 rolls on 20-inch wheels.

Cost? The equivalent of a Dr. Evil-approved $1 million, plus you have to B.Y.O. Gallardo. That is an astounding amount of money for a restomod version of a non-V-12 Lamborghini, and it seems almost like a litmus test of just how much the restomod market boom will bear. Only 25 examples are planned to be built.

Perhaps more interestingly, Cavën Atelier positions the Hommage 26 not as a one-off, but as a potential series of cars that will see designers return to their work for upcoming reboots. The options here are endless: Ken Okuyama redoes the Ferrari Enzo? A Camilo Pardo reworked first-generation Ford GT? Even if the price tags will be out of reach, the designs will be interesting to see.

In the meantime, your standard Gallardo remains pretty accessible for how exotic it looks and drives. Put aside a little money for an Akrapovič exhaust, and enjoy it just as Giugiaro originally imagined.

SHT Days 18, 19, 20: Plugging along SOBO, some SOLO

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Yep, today was a nasty weather day. Having made my decision to back off miles a bit, Bill and I decided to sightsee more by car, take a short day-hike, and make a stop at another brewery.

We drove north of Grand Marais on Highway 61 (a scenic drive on its own) to Judge Magney State Park. I wanted Bill to see Devil’s Kettle Falls. I had been here last year, on that fateful day of my knee injury. Here’s a link to my final post on that day from last year- https://thetrek.co/sht-day-7-journey-to-the-center-of-the-earth/ I wanted to see it again as well, to remember it on better terms.

We piled on some layers, including our rain jackets, and hiked up the mile from the trailhead. The falls were just as cool as I remember, one half disappearing into oblivion, and the other side cascading down toward Lake Superior. Then again were the stairs, though they were much easier to tackle this time around, with a functioning knee and no backpack. We took our pictures and admired the falls together before returning to the trailhead.

We decided to while away the afternoon at Voyageur Brewing Company in Grand Marais. It was pretty busy. It seems everyone else in the area had the same idea to escape the weather. Families with children were even here, parents playing games with their kids at the tables.

The brewery in Grand Marais. I’ve been here before, on a trip to Isle Royale National Park.

We had a few drinks and appetizers, and enjoyed some local, northern Minnesota grub. I made use of the Wi-Fi to help my parents with an issue back at home, and eventually we headed back to camp.

Sampling the local fare: smoked whitefish from just a few feet away in Lake Superior.

I forgot to mention that we had moved to a new campground at this point. I had made a reservation well in advance for Cascade State Park, and we got ourselves packed up and moved there last night. I had reserved this campsite assuming I’d be moving along northward on the SHT, and while I was no longer doing that exactly, we absolutely loved Cascade State Park and it was a great decision to stay here for the remainder of our vacation.

Today the weather was marginally better than yesterday, but I was itching to get back on trail again. I planned to move SOBO on trail from here on, to close the gap between Judge Magney State Park and the Highway 1 trailhead near Tettegouche State Park.

This meant that today I would be re-doing a few miles from Judge Magney south toward Grand Marais. There was no way around it, as last year I bailed out on a private road. No matter, it’s just a few miles, and if my memory serves me correctly, with a properly working knee the miles should be easy.

Bill wanted to hike with me today as the trail makes its one and only trip to the beach of Lake Superior in this section. We shuttled one vehicle to the trailhead on County Road 14, and together headed farther north back to Judge Magney. The first couple of miles truly were easy, and memories from last year were flooding back. Bill couldn’t believe I hiked this far on a bum knee. He remembers how much pain I was in when I came home last year.

I loved the vibrant green moss on the trail today.

The low-lying vegetation was the star of the show this morning.

Eventually we popped out of the trees and crossed Highway 61. Just on the other side was the beach! There was Lake Superior in all her glory. She was angry today though, splashing cold waves and mist into the gray skies. We had lunch on some rocks on the shoreline. We had the brilliant idea to heat up some chicken noodle soup and bring it in a thermos today. Man, did that hit the spot on a day like today!

And there she is! Lake Superior! I’ve been seeing it in the distance for 200 miles now, and finally get to walk next to it.

Reaching to touch the water, just to say I did. There’s no way any more than a hand is getting in there today. Lake Superior is freezing cold all year round.

Our cozy lunch spot, enjoying hot soup and watching the waves.

Today was not a good day to enjoy the lake, as it was so cold and windy, but it did make for some amazing waves. We enjoyed watching and hearing them roar as we had lunch, and walked along the beach portion of the SHT for the next 1.5 miles. To be honest, this beach walk was the most difficult mileage of the day. The loose pebbles made it hard for our footsteps to make purchase, and I was happy to return to firm ground.

Retreating inland to the quiet and relative warmth of the woods, we continued on. We briefly climbed up away from the lake and highway as we made our way to the Kadunce River. We crossed the river on a nice bridge and finished out the last few miles to retrieve our vehicle. Today was 11 total miles from the large trailhead at Judge Magney to the tiny trailhead off Cook County Road 14.

Today, under better weather conditions, we were going to finish the miles to the town of Grand Marais.  We parked a vehicle at the large Pincushion Trailhead just outside of Grand Marais and together drove north back to County Road 14. Together we hiked south back to Pincushion.

We crossed a couple of damaged bridges to start our day, and the first four miles were incredibly rooty and rocky. I was actually glad Bill got to experience this, as roots and rocks were pretty normal for this trail. He’s had fairly easy terrain on his day hikes with me so far. Now he knows what it’s usually like out here!

The bridge is out over Cliff Creek. I did not even attempt to cross this one since the creek is so low and I could just hop across it.

Another bridge out over Durfee Creek. Despite the massive tree on top, the bridge looked pretty structurally sound and we both gingerly walked across it anyway.

We gently climbed for a few miles up and over Wildflower Hill before coming right back down alongside Woods Creek. We had lunch at the Woods Creek Campsite before entering a wonderful forest of giant pines after the Lindskog Road Trailhead. It was as if we entered a new country. The scenery in here was incredible. Combined with the views of the Devil Track River, and this afternoon’s hiking was truly amazing.

The brand new, just finished days ago, bridge over the Devil Track River.

Catching a glimpse of falls through the trees.

I still had gas left in the tank, so to speak, so after we reached Pincushion, I decided to tackle some additional mileage while the getting was good. I had Bill shuttle me over to where the trail crosses Old Ski Hill Road and I hiked BACK (NOBO) to Pincushion Trailhead again. This way I could take advantage of going downhill (great decision!), and Bill could run to the grocery store in town for dinner provisions. Just as I was crossing the Gunflint Trail, which is a bit of a misnomer as it is a major paved highway out of Grand Marais, Bill happened to drive by on his way back to Pincushion! What timing! He honked at me as I waved back, stunned. In short order, I finished those extra two miles and we called it a day. 14 miles in total today, 12 with Bill and 2 bonus miles solo.

My name is Misty, and I hail from Nebraska, where I live with my husband, two cats, and flock of chickens. I worked in healthcare for over 16 years before giving it up for more outdoor pursuits. I hope to have you follow along on my new adventures!

Notify me via e-mail if anyone answers my comment.

Hulick also revealed that he was asked by BioWare to score an internal demo for Mass Effect: Universe—but work was cut short by the studio's closure.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Mass Effect composer Sam Hulick revealed in October that he was 're-scoring' the leaked trailer for Mass Effect: Universe to reflect his own "musical vision" for the game. The original, with an edit of "Quartz" by French 79, is pretty groovy, but now Hulick's work is done and this may not come as much of a surprise but I think I like his version better.

So here we have it—Mass Effect: Universe - Sam Hulick Edit:

I'm not a music critic but I know what I like, and I really like the Mass Effect soundtracks. There's a broadly common sound threaded through all three games (I didn't play Andromeda so I'm just pretending it never happened) but they evolved over the course of the trilogy, too: Hulick told me his music in this trailer is "'a balancing act between paying homage to the original trilogy and exploring new ground", but leans most heavily toward Mass Effect 1 and 3.

"Mass Effect's earliest tracks were more about raw analog synth blended with orchestral elements, but more subdued and 'vibey' for lack of a better word," Hulick said in a recent chat with PC Gamer. "Mass Effect 2 leaned more into orchestral bombast (as with Jack Wall’s forever-famous 'Suicide Mission'), and Mass Effect 3 combined the best of both worlds, with plenty of epic, fist-pump-into-the-air orchestral moments, as well as sparse, moody synth-forward tracks like the ones I wrote for Mars."

Jack Wall, for the record, was lead composer on the original Mass Effect while Hulick served as co-composer; the Mass Effect 2 soundtrack brought back Wall and Hulick and added Jimmy Hinson and David Kates to the lineup, while Mass Effect 3 featured Hulick plus Sascha Dikiciyan, Christopher Lennertz, Clint Mansell, and Cris Velasco.

"I would've loved to have actual songs (with lyrics) to elevate the more emotional moments in the game, whatever they might've been. Fans loved the ME1 end-credits song by Faunts, so why not bring more of that back?"

The passage of time is also a factor in Hulick's approach to new work on Mass Effect. The Citadel DLC for Mass Effect 3 came out in 2013, 13 years ago, and he hasn't been sitting idle since then. "I used to rely on synth presets, but these days I mostly create my own sounds from scratch, since it's faster than scrolling through a list of 1,000 premade sounds," Hulick said. "On top of that, the quality of my music production has definitely improved since 2013! Everything in the trailer re-score should sound more polished, but still feel like it belongs in the Mass Effect universe (no pun intended)."

There are some big stylistic shifts over the course of the trailer, made even more notable by its brevity, which Hulick said are meant to showcase different approaches to ambient, cinematic, and combat music—"paying homage" to the original scores while simultaneously exploring new ground. The fight scene near the end of the teaser, for instance, is where Hulick "let the synthwave side come out," while the rest of the music is more in keeping with the music of the original trilogy.

Keep up to date with the most important stories and the best deals, as picked by the PC Gamer team.

Hulick also shared his "pipe dream" for the Mass Effect: Universe soundtrack, had he been hired to compose it: "I would've loved to have actual songs (with lyrics) to elevate the more emotional moments in the game, whatever they might've been. Fans loved the ME1 end-credits song by Faunts, so why not bring more of that back and include it in the game?"

I think that's a spectacular idea because he's absolutely correct, we did love that shit. It hits hard—still one of my favorite game tracks ever.

The interesting (and sad) surprise is that all of this isn't entirely a flight of fancy. BioWare had reached out to Hulick about working with the studio on an internal demo for a new Mass Effect: No promises beyond that were made and BioWare was clear that it hadn't begun the process of choosing a composer for the full game, but naturally Hulick said yes to "the amazing opportunity to write and brainstorm with the team on the new musical direction."

Sadly, it never got further than that: Hulick said he was "just starting to build a sonic palette" when he learned, along with the rest of us, that BioWare was being closed.

"Had I been hired to work on Mass Effect Universe, I'd have liked to see the direction lean more toward heavy analog synth while also introducing elements of synthwave. I don't think it would've been 100% synth, though. To remove all orchestral elements from the Mass Effect world feels like it would really hobble some of the more emotional peaks in the narrative," Hulic said.

"That's not to say synthesizer-centric music can't strike people emotionally, of course. But things like solo piano and orchestral arrangements have been part of the soul of Mass Effect's score since day one, and without them, I don't know if the music would feel complete."

In a separate post on his Patreon, Hulick paid tribute to BioWare and the impact Mass Effect had on his career: "BioWare took a chance on a young, unproven composer—an incredible opportunity that wouldn't have happened if Jack Wall hadn't recommended me during their composer search. Working on the first three games helped me not only find my musical voice, but also develop the skills needed to work on major video game projects."

He also repeated the sentiment he expressed on Bluesky when BioWare's shutdown was announced: As bad as it is for gamers, it's much worse for the people who work there. "It goes without saying that while we contractors feel the pain of studios shutting down, no one has been more impacted by this shutdown than the hard-working, kind, talented people at BioWare. Please, if you have connections in the game industry, leverage them to help everyone land on their feet."

Absolutely heartbroken over the end of an era. I hope all the talented and wonderful folks at BioWare land on their feet and keep working on incredible games. 💔 I loved every minute of working with you on the Mass Effect trilogy.

Following the now-confirmed end of Dragon Age in 2025, former BioWare senior writer Sheryl Chee said EA owns the IP, but it can't own the idea: "DA isn't dead because it's yours now." It's small consolation, especially coming so soon after BioWare's closure, but I think the same has to be said about Mass Effect. We may not get more from EA, but there will always be more from us. Happy N7 Day.

GOG Galaxy—the DRM-free storefront's go at a desktop client and launcher—was always a nice dream that didn't measure up in reality. The pitch was: all your games, from all your PC game storefronts, in one centralised hub, but it never left beta in its 2.0 form and, in general, ran pretty poorly compared to its most obvious competition (Steam).

But that was old GOG. New, independent GOG has big plans for Galaxy. In the storefront's first "GOG Showcase", released Thursday, it announced the launcher would be getting a revamp in 2027, and it's coming to Linux too. "We know this has been a long time coming, but we wanted to do it properly: GOG Galaxy is officially heading to Linux", says the studio.

"At the same time, we’ve reworked the client on Windows and Mac to be simpler, run faster, and give you cleaner ways to view your library. Most importantly, our core promise stays untouched: GOG Galaxy is entirely optional. Your games are yours to download and keep through offline installers, exactly as they’ve always been".

That is to say, you'll still be able to find offline game installers in your GOG library if you want to prep for the digital ownership apocalypse (which, let me be clear, is probably a wise move).

Of course, if you're someone who plays games on Linux (cool people, I believe is the technical term), then you likely already have perhaps several tools which do the GOG Galaxy thing of letting you configure and install games from multiple storefronts: programs like Heroic, Faugus, or Lutris. Still, it'd be good to have a ship-shape Galaxy on multiple platforms, and a store with GOG's stature getting in on Linux is good news for PC gaming in general.

The Linuxification of everything isn't the only bit of news GOG released at its showcase. It also announced GOG Social: "a quiet, tidy hangout for actual game talk", meant to centralise discussion and socialising around "screenshot galleries, videos, and community polls with threaded replies and zero algorithmic noise".

If either of those things sounds cool to you, you can sign up for their respective betas over at GOG.

Send your name to Saturn's moon Titan with NASA's upcoming Dragonfly mission

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

NASA is asking the public to send their names to Titan for an ambitious mission that will see a flying craft explore the Saturn moon in 2034, if all goes according to plan.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

We still may be some time away from sending humans to distant reaches of the solar system, but you can send your name to Saturn's moon Titan, which has the ingredients of life on its surface thanks to NASA's Dragonfly mission.

Dragonfly is an SUV-sized nuclear-powered rotorcraft that NASA aims to launch towards Titan in 2028. The agency opened a special events page in late October to allow members of the public to put their name on the spacecraft. The names will be put upon a chip on Dragonfly during the spacecraft's assembly, and all names must be submitted before Jan. 15, 2027.

The agency says that the mission has no "ordinary itinerary," as Dragonfly aims to between areas miles (or kilometers) apart after arriving on the surface in 2034. "Nearly a billion miles away, an extraordinary destination awaits. Titan has an atmosphere, rivers and lakes, seasons, sweeping dunes and a landscape rich in organic material," NASA officials wrote in the event page. "Upon arrival in 2034, Dragonfly will fly from place to place on this intriguing world to uncover clues about this remarkable moon, and the chemistry that could help us understand the ingredients for life."

Dragonfly will be one of just a few flying crafts on another world, with the first being NASA's plucky Ingenuity mini-helicopter that arrived on Mars along with the still-running Perseverance rover in 2021. Ingenuity was only designed to make five flights, but made it to 72 until its blades were damaged during a landing on Jan. 18, 2024.

NASA is now planning a SkyFall mission on Mars that would use three helicopters, and which may launch as soon as late 2028 for the Red Planet.

Dragonfly, however, is not the first mission to Titan: that would be Huygens, which operated for just a few hours on Jan. 14, 2005 after deploying from the Cassini spacecraft. Huygens was the first lander on an outer solar system world, and measured Titan's atmosphere and took data from the surface.

For its part, Cassini flew by Titan more than 100 times before the mission concluded in September 2017, providing valuable long-term data about the moon from above. Dragonfly aims to show the moon from up close for about 3.3 Earth years. Its destinations will include dunes and a young impact crater called Selk, according to NASA.

"Dragonfly is expected to make one flight every one to two Titan days, which is called a Tsol and lasts about 16 Earth days," NASA officials added of the mission. Dragonfly will stop at a variety of geologic sites, where it will collect samples of surface material for analysis inside the rotorcraft by a suite of scientific instruments."

Dragonfly's goals include learning more about prebiotic chemistry, or the conditions prior to life arising in a location; figuring out if Titan had habitable conditions in the past; and looking for "chemical indicators" of life based on water or hydrocarbons.

Elizabeth Howell (she/her), Ph.D., was a staff writer in the spaceflight channel between 2022 and 2024 specializing in Canadian space news. She was contributing writer for Space.com for 10 years from 2012 to 2024. Elizabeth's reporting includes multiple exclusives with the White House, leading world coverage about a lost-and-found space tomato on the International Space Station, witnessing five human spaceflight launches on two continents, flying parabolic, working inside a spacesuit, and participating in a simulated Mars mission.
