However, cryptocurrency holdings are generally not covered by traditional bank deposit insurance. Fintech platforms may also use partner banks, so customers should identify the institution actually holding their deposits and verify the applicable protection.

Harbinger scores another $240M in latest investment round

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Electric truck maker Harbinger has been racking up wins in recent months, and after scoring a $300 million order from international shipping giant FedEx and a defense contract with the US military, the company has drummed up another $240 million in Series D funding.

Companies like FedEx and Amazon have been making serious moves to fully electrify their fleet operations, both in an effort to decarbonize and to avoid the every-increasing price of diesel. At $300 million for 2,000 medium-duty electric delivery trucks, Harbinger’s latest deal with FedEx is believed to be one of the largest binding orders for electric medium or heavy-duty trucks in history – and it’s a big enough deal to inspire more than 240 million votes of confidence in the company’s future, one dollar at a time.

“Harbinger exists to challenge what industry has learned to accept, and reinvent the critical systems the world depends on,” said John Harris, the Co-Founder and CEO of Harbinger. “By rethinking critical systems from first principles, we aim to unlock better performance, greater reliability, safer operations, and more room to grow. Electrification is at the core of everything we do, and increasingly, electrification is at the core of industry, defense, mobility, and AI. Harbinger’s role is not simply to give customers another option. It is to reset expectations for what critical systems should deliver.”

Harbinger’s Series D funding round was co-led by Tiger Global, the Capricorn Technology Impact Fund, and Ridgeline. The company says the new funding will support expansion into energy storage systems, power generation, autonomy software, and defense applications, as well as the continued ramp up of its medium-duty platform manufacturing capacity. With this latest round of financing, Harbinger has raised over $650 million since 2021.

As for me, I’m hoping they use the money to come up with more fun, high-octane chase videos that show off their trucks’ performance capabilities. Time will tell.

Three Zelda: Ocarina of Times later, Eiji Aonuma is finally happy with the infamous Water Temple

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

One of the most infamous dungeons in The Legend of Zelda's history is undoubtedly Ocarina of Time's Water Temple. But after three Ocarina of Times and two full remakes, series producer Eiji Aonuma is finally happy with how the dungeon turned out in the new Switch 2 version.

Asked if he's finally content with the Water Temple in The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time's Switch 2 remake, while speaking to Game Informer, the series lead simply said "certainly." Aonuma acknowledged that the original N64 Water Temple "was hard," but some of that trickiness came from the "system that we were presenting to players."

1998's Water Temple had players equipping and then slipping off a pair of iron boots that would make Link sink or float up in these rooms full of water, hence the name. The headache came from the actual act of wearing the boots, since you'd have to drudge through the menus every single time. Thankfully, the Switch 2 version takes some notes from the 3DS remake and retains simple button mapping for items.

"Now that we're able to give players a shortcut, single button on the gameplay screen, I think they can get to that more quickly. It's a better experience because they can focus on solving the puzzle rather than having to navigate menus and thinking about that and interrupting the flow of their thought each time," Aonuma explains.

The Zelda lead also notes that a more fluid camera makes all the difference, especially when you're under water or in tight spaces: "Another consideration compared to the original is that you couldn't move the camera angle in the original game, and so if you were looking for a certain room in this 3D space relative to where you are now, it was much more difficult to orient yourself and think about the space where that room might exist. So I think those two together, you know, also with the heavy boots being one button press, I think this, for me, is the best version of the Water Temple."

There you have it, folks - the Switch 2's Water Temple is officially the best Water Temple of them all, at least, according to Aonuma.

We might see more controversial Nintendo levels get the same treatment, as an executive recently teased more remakes alongside original games. Producer Hidemaro Fujibayashi also stressed the importance of all-new ideas in the Zelda series, however.

Storytelling Makes the Hiking Miles Fly By

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

We got an early start at 7:00 AM under a grey sky and cool temperatures hovering in the high 50s. A thick morning fog embraced us as we descended the hillside, crossing a different bridge than the one we had used to enter Portomarin yesterday.

Immediately after crossing the river, we entered into a forested trail that launched into a very steep climb. We had a lot of uphill work ahead of us today. Once again, we found ourselves walking along ancient dirt paths framed with old stone walls and rich, dark soil.

This forest was extraordinarily lush, dense, and beautiful. With mosses and ferns blanketing the ground, it felt very similar to sections of the Appalachian Trail. Pines dominated the canopy, interspersed with hardwoods.

The rest of the morning followed a steady, gradual ascent across a mix of dirt paths and paved country roads. Eventually, the trail ran parallel to a busy highway dotted with active farms. The strong smell of manure and squealing from farm buildings suggested we were passing large pig livestock operations.

We made our first stop at a busy cafe in the small hamlet of Castromajor, which was packed with fellow hikers. Although the quiet intimacy of the Camino prior to Sarria was gone, we had mentally prepared for this shift. Leaving the hamlet, the trail veered away from the highway onto a wide dirt road bounded by four-foot-tall rock walls, continuing its steady upward climb.

The walk felt somewhat nondescript, muted by the constant lines of loud hikers ahead of and behind us. However, we passed through several charming villages, including one where a volunteer group from Germany was stamping pilgrim passports and offering free coffee. Collecting at least two stamps per day is mandatory during this final 100-kilometer stretch to qualify for the Compostela certificate in Santiago.

After coffee, we reconnected with our tramily. The miles flew by as we entertained each other with stories. In fact, they flew by too fast—just as Robert was wrapping up a fascinating life story, Jill realized they had accidently passed their albergue for the night! They said their goodbyes and doubled back.

Shortly after parting ways, the throngs of hikers suddenly dissipated. For the first time all day, the trail ahead of us was completely empty, with only a handful of walkers visible behind us. Most of the crowds had evidently reached their daily destinations and turned in.

We pushed on for two more miles to Palas de Rei, a small city built on a steep hill. Our accommodation was a comfortable apartment situated directly above a local bar. After logging 16 miles, we were thoroughly exhausted.

For dinner we decided on pizza. Since we were in Galicia, I ordered mine topped with fresh seafood. We both left the restaurant with a firm conclusion: Spanish pizza is a very different experience than traditional Italian pizza!

After a quick grocery run, we returned to our room, enjoyed a glass of wine, and fell asleep by “hiker midnight” (7:30 p.m.). We have finally mastered the Spanish rolling window shutters (persianas). These heavy metal blinds block heat and provide security, allowing us to leave the glass window cracked behind them to let in the cool night air. Although noises from late-night partygoers occasionally drifted up from the street, we were far tired and drifted back to sleep.

The buyer pays the same price as they would otherwise, and your purchase helps to support The Trek's ongoing goal to serve you quality backpacking advice and information. Thanks for your support!

To learn more, please visit the About This Site page.

After decades of no backpacking an abrupt life incident forced me back into the woods for some shinrin-yoku where I ran into a NOBO tramily at Shuckstack Fire Tower. That started an idea of a longer term forest bath on the AT. What a long strange trip its been. Any financial donations I get will be used to send out love to other thru hikers a trail magic.

Atmos Rewards Summit Card 3x Points On Foreign Purchases: Any Catch?

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

The $395 annual fee Atmos™ Rewards Summit Visa Infinite® Credit Card (learn more) is the premium personal credit card of Atmos Rewards, which is the combined loyalty program of Alaska & Hawaiian.

Specifically, I’m talking about how the card earns 3x points on all foreign purchases, with no caps, which is a pretty unheard of bonus category.

Link: Learn more about the Atmos™ Rewards Summit Visa Infinite® Credit Card

The Atmos Rewards Summit Card offers 3x points on eligible foreign transactions, with no caps. This applies to both the primary cardmember and to any authorized users (authorized users can be added to the card at no extra cost). Specifically, eligible foreign purchases include:

In the interest of being thorough, let me copy and paste the portion of the terms addressing this:

3 Points for eligible Foreign Transactions: Earn 3 points for every $1 spent on eligible foreign transaction Net Purchases (1) made in a foreign currency, or (2) made in U.S. dollars if the transaction is made or processed outside of the United States and US Territories. Bank of America is not responsible for how foreign transactions are billed to the card.

Let me emphasize that the card has no foreign transaction fees, so not only are you not paying those fees, but you’re being rewarded very handsomely. The exchange rates used by the card are also totally fair, and competitive.

Personally, I value Atmos Rewards points at 1.5 cents each, so I’d consider that to be a 4.5% return on all foreign purchases, which is unrivaled. If you have an eligible Bank of America account, you can even get a 10% relationship bonus, meaning you could net 3.3x points per dollar spent.

While there are some cards that might be slightly more rewarding for specific categories of foreign spending — like dining, airfare, or hotels — there’s not another card that’s broadly this rewarding for all foreign spending.

Keep in mind that this is only the start of the rewards you get for spending on the card:

Admittedly this is a very specific situation, but if you were to spend $60,000 abroad, you’d earn 180,000 Atmos Rewards points, 30,000 Atmos Rewards status points (not including the 10,000 status points boost the card offers annually), and a 100,000-point Global Companion Award.

If you want to take it a step further, those 40,000 Atmos Rewards status points are enough to earn Atmos Rewards Gold status, which gets you oneworld Sapphire, offering perks like priority services and lounge access. That’s a pretty incredible setup, eh?

As you’d expect, there are a lot of questions about the Atmos Rewards Summit Card 3x points on foreign spending:

“I’ve looked but I couldn’t find what the terms are for the 3x points on foreign purchases. Is there an annual limit? Does it need to be combined with domestic spend?”

“Let me get this straight – any transaction outside the US will be in the 3x category? Is this capped? As someone living in Europe, that could make this card extremely valuable for me…”

Officially, there are no limits on how much you can spend abroad. Of course to get this card, you need to be eligible for credit cards in the United States, which requires having an address in the country, among other things.

As I look at the terms, there’s nothing there stating any limits as to how much you can spend abroad, both in absolute and relative (to domestic spending) terms. In practice, there are plenty of Americans living abroad who use US credit cards (given how much more lucrative they are than most foreign cards) without any issues.

So I don’t think there’s a catch here, but I will say this (and I’m not trying to fear monger, but am just trying to address the only potential concern) — banks can shut down your credit cards at will, and decide they no longer want to do business with you.

In the past, I’ve read reports where credit card issuers have shut down accounts because too big of a percentage of spending was consistently abroad. That’s far from consistent, though, and I’ve also heard of people using their card living abroad for a decade without an issue.

My point is to say that based on how things currently stand, I think this benefit is as good as it sounds, and I wouldn’t expect any major changes (though who knows, maybe at some point down the road we’ll see a cap on how much foreign spending can earn bonus points).

Many people may wonder if this is sustainable in the long run, so let me point out a few things:

My point is simply to say that this doesn’t strike me anywhere near the top of the list of unsustainable credit card benefits, though we’ll see how this plays out.

The Atmos Rewards Summit Card offers 3x points on foreign spending, with no foreign transaction fees and no caps. This is an unheard of bonus category, and makes this by far the best card for foreign spending.

What makes this even better is that beyond the 3x points, you also earn one status point for every $2 spent, and if you spend $60,000 in an anniversary year, you can receive a 100,000-point Global Companion Award. This return on spending is every bit as great as it sounds, and this is the card to have in your wallet if you travel abroad a lot.

Troublesome black bear Nugget wins the 2026 Annoying Sibling Award

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 7, 2026 12:32 PM EST

Anyone who has brothers and sisters knows that siblings can be your greatest allies and biggest foes all at once. The same principle appears to apply to bear siblings.

A small black bear (Ursus americanus) named Nugget demonstrated that all too well this summer. Nugget is known for his very small stature, a fur pattern that looks like goggles around his eyes, and a reddish coat that shifts to black later in the summer. He also likes to get up in his siblings business.

Over the summer, the U.S. Forest Service team at Tongass National Forest in southeastern Alaska watched Nugget jabbing at his very patient and bigger sibling Crescent. For these antics, Nugget officially won the 2026 Annoying Sibling Award.

“This scene went on for almost 30 minutes. Each time Nugget would slowly creep out and touch Crescent and then they’d seem to play fight,” the rangers write. “It was like watching two siblings fight until the bigger sibling had enough. Well managed ecosystems and recreation sites on national forests like the Tongass provide unique opportunities to witness scenes like this.”

The rangers at Tongass National Forest are currently awarding the Anan Bear Awards to the 39 bears spotted at the Anan Wildlife Observatory in Alaska over the summer.

Tongass is the largest national forest in the United States, and covers most of southeastern Alaska, including the state’s capital city of Juneau. The area includes Mendanhall Glacier (a popular destination for Alaskan cruises), hiking trails, eagles soaring above, and plenty of bears. One of the hotspots is Anan Creek, home to one of the largest runs of pink salmon in Southeast Alaska. The run is a very popular dinner spot among black and brown bears.

According to Friends of Anan, 39 bears are known to feast on the salmon at Anan Creek. The population includes 31 black bears and eight brown bears (Ursus arctos). Like their chunky counterparts farther to the north in Katmai National Park, the bears return each summer to feast on salmon and teach their cubs how to fish in order to survive. And also like the fat bears of Katmai, every bear here has their own habits and quirks.

The U.S. Forest Service tracks and documents every bear spotted at Anan, while explore.org’s cameras broadcast live streams of what they are up to 24/7.

Yamaha’s new electric scooter takes aim at Europe’s 125cc commuters

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Yamaha is expanding its electric scooter lineup with the new XENTER E, a 125cc-equivalent electric scooter designed specifically with European city riding in mind.

Unveiled around EICMA 2026 in Milan alongside the new NMAX E, the XENTER E is a high-wheel scooter riding on 16-inch wheels front and rear.

That’s an important distinction in European cities, where larger wheels can make quick work of the cobblestones, potholes, and tram tracks that can turn smaller-wheeled scooters into teeth-rattling experiences.

The XENTER E uses Yamaha’s new eAxle powertrain, combining the motor, controller, and reduction gearbox into a compact frame-mounted unit. The motor produces 7.5 kW (10 hp) of peak power and 33.3 Nm of torque, putting the scooter into Europe’s A1 license category.

Mounting the motor to the frame also means Yamaha can use a more conventional motorcycle-style swingarm rather than hanging the motor on the rear suspension like many scooters. That reduces unsprung weight and should help improve handling and ride quality.

Power is supplied from an integrated 3.5 kWh battery mounted low in the chassis. Yamaha claims 108 km (67 miles) of range on the WMTC Class 1 cycle, though real-world range is expected to be somewhat lower.

An 800W onboard charger can refill the battery in around 5.5 hours from a public charging station. Yamaha will also offer a cable for charging from a household outlet.

Other niceties include three riding modes, traction control, regenerative braking, a reverse mode for maneuvering at low speeds, smart-key operation, and a 5-inch TFT display. Smartphone connectivity adds navigation, battery information, maintenance reminders, and parking-location tracking.

There’s also enough underseat storage for a full-face helmet, depending on its size, plus USB-C charging and provisions for accessories such as a windscreen and 34-liter top case.

The XENTER E shares its powertrain and battery with Yamaha’s new NMAX E, but the high-wheel layout gives it a distinctly more European commuter flavor.

Yamaha hasn’t officially announced pricing for each market, though early reports from Milan point to €7,799 in Germany. Deliveries are expected to begin in spring 2027.

It’s certainly not cheap, but the XENTER E looks like Yamaha is finally getting serious about building electric scooters that can replace its bread-and-butter 125cc urban machines, rather than simply supplement them.

Xbox is testing a way to use your phone as a controller on smart TVs

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Xbox is testing two new ways to play its cloud games, and one of them doesn't require a controller at all.

Announced on Xbox Wire on 4th November, the phone controller preview is rolling out on supported TVs wherever Xbox Cloud Gaming is available. The phone option - which works with both Android and iOS devices - lets you use your phone "as a controller when you don't have one nearby". Connect to your TV via QR code, and the controls open in your phone's browser.

The second preview, called cloud connect, is currently limited to the US and lets the new Xbox Nano controller talk to your cloud session over wi- fi instead of pairing directly with your TV to "help reduce input latency, so gameplay feels more responsive".

To try out the phone controls yourself, switch on Preview Features under Settings > Account in the latest version of the Xbox smart TV app.

Xbox has published a setup guide with full instructions, although you should note that both experiences "are still being tested", so the availability and supported games or devices "may be limited while [Xbox] learns from your feedback".

ICYMI, as of yesterday, anyone in the Xbox Insider Program can try Microsoft's updated Xbox PC app. The company claims it now boots up 60% faster, several pages can launch up to 50 percent faster, and in-app windows can open up to 65 percent faster.

The 2-Minute Lard Trick That Helps Smaller Songbirds Get Their Fair Share of Food in Fall and Winter

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Beautiful plants and ideas to brighten your garden.

Expert tips for growing fruit, vegetables and herbs at home.

Sign up to get fresh gardening ideas, inspiration and expert advice delivered straight to your inbox.

Do You Have America’s Most Bird-Friendly Backyard? Enter for a Chance to Win a Birdfy Smart Feeder

8 Easy Landscape Designer Tricks to Transition Your Garden From Fall into Winter

Sorry Squirrels, This Simple Trick Stops Pests Eating Tulip Bulbs (and it Beats Chicken Wire Every Time)

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Smearing fat onto tree bark mimics natural foraging to help smaller nuthatches, wrens, and chickadees feast safely while keeping bully-boy starlings at bay.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

If you're feeding garden birds this fall, you'll know that fat balls and suet feeders are often mobbed by dominant birds like starlings and grackles, leaving nothing for more timid feathered friends to feast upon – which is why you should also smear a little lard on a tree or fencepost. Adding fat to your bird feeding buffet is essential as temperatures fall, but small birds who need this energy-boost most often miss out. However, this quick bird-feeding trick ensures smaller, shyer nuthatches, chickadees, titmice, wrens and creepers get their fair share of high-energy fuel, because bigger birds are unable to cling to this vertical surface to feed.

Smearing lard, tallow or vegetable shortening onto a tree only takes two minutes4 but this simple addition really can make a difference to how smaller garden songbirds fare in winter. The smaller a bird is, the more vulnerable it is to freezing or starving as temperatures drop, and the more likely it is to be bullied from feeders by more aggressive birds. This fat-smear trick bypasses those bullying birds, mimicking natural foraging to tempt even cautious birds like brown creepers who are far too shy to use regular feeders.

If you like to feed birds in winter, tree-smeared lard can attract unusual visitors like woodpeckers to your yard as insects become scarce, and you'll also enjoy watching nuthatches walk headfirst down the tree trunk to enjoy their fat feast. Smearing lard on a tree also avoids the danger of smaller birds getting their tongues, claws or legs tangled in a fat-ball mesh holder. You can also easily mix birdseed into the fat to provide extra nutrients, or try our super-tasty bark butter recipe below.

Any tree or fencepost will do, but aim for a more mature tree or older fencepost that has furrows or cracks that will hold more lard and give clinging birds a foothold. Take a golf-ball sized chunk of lard, tallow or vegetable shortening and press it onto the wood, ideally 5-6 feet high, on the shady side of the tree or post so it doesn’t melt in the sun.

Mix in a pinch of cayenne pepper to deter squirrels if needs be, as they’ll quickly learn to leave it alone while birds are unaffected by the taste and heat. Only smear enough fat on the tree for birds to finish during daylight hours to avoid attracting nocturnal scavengers like racoons and opossums. And if you live in bear country, wait until late November or December when they're in full hibernation before trying this trick.

You can use animal-based lard or tallow, and plant-based vegetable shortening to feed garden birds in winter using this quick trick.

Lard is cheap, full of fast energy for birds and has a soft, buttery texture so is easily pressed into bark furrows. Look for a product that’s 100% pure lard such as South Chicago Packing Lard, available from Amazon. You’ll find similar products at most grocery stores but read the ingredients list carefully: while you don’t need to worry about citric acid, avoid synthetic stabilizers like propyl gallate, BHT or BHA, commonly added to grocery-store lard to prevent it going rancid. Don't use products with added salt or sodium as birds can't process it and high levels can be fatal.

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Tallow is simply suet in rendered form and, again, you need to look for a product that’s 100% tallow such as Farmer Bob’s 100% Beef Tallow, available from Amazon. It has a similar consistency to lard and has a higher nutritional value as well as being a great source of energy.

Vegetable shortening is less nutritious but it still provides a valuable high-energy boost to small songbirds in cold conditions. For regular use, an organic all-vegetable shortening such as Spectrum Naturals Organic Shortening, available from Walmart is best. Unrefined and free of synthetic preservatives, it retains some nutrients and is firm enough to use for this trick. It's fine to use Crisco, also available from Walmart, if that’s what you already have in your panty, but it’s less nutritious and melts more easily so think of it as an occasional junk-food treat for birds.

In the North, Midwest and Mountain West, temperatures are cold enough to simply smear lard or tallow straight onto the tree or post without it melting or spoiling in fall and winter. Vegetable shortening will need mixing with cornmeal to stiffen it up before using. Of course, you can still add ingredients to lard and tallow to increase the nutritional value.

In the South and Southwest, mild fall and winter days can be warm enough to soften lard, tallow and vegetable shortening, so mix with cornmeal to form a firmer dough before using.

While plain lard smeared onto a tree or fencepost works just fine, this no-cook recipe will tempt even the shyest feeders. Mixing a handful of natural, raw ingredients into the fat also creates a pliable dough that’s the perfect consistency to press into bark furrows in all zones.

Providing food for small, shy songbirds in this way is a useful part of a bird-food buffet in fall and winter. The more ways you can offer food, the better, from planting berried shrubs that provide a natural feast for winter birds to saving kitchen scraps for your feathered friends. Different species prefer different food types and also to feed in various ways, so using a range of bird feeders to satisfy different species is a smart move, too. Just don't forget to cater for the smallest backyard birds with this quick lard-smear trick.

As a keen natural crafter, her cutting patch and veg bed are increasingly being taken over by plants that can be dried or woven into a crafty project.

The Bantu expansion changed Africa. Scientists are still tracing how.

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Genes and archaeology are revealing how Bantu speakers spread across much of Africa.

It’s not easy to extract iron from its ore. But the peoples of the ancient city of Great Zimbabwe—the capital of a thriving kingdom in southern Africa between 1000 to 1700 AD—were masters.

At sites near the city’s stone ruins, clusters of iron-smelting furnaces survive today as mounds of rubble. At Mashava, a village 25 miles northwest, fragments bear fingermarks of those who long ago smoothed the structures’ clay interiors.

Some of the furnaces were nearly an adult’s arm span in width, with walls thick and strong to contain the heat of more than 2,100° Fahrenheit needed to smelt iron. Unlike other furnaces that required workers to force air into them with bellows, these were designed to suck air in, then out through a chimney, no human labor required. This would have made it significantly easier to produce the iron, which was fashioned into hoes, axes, and arrowheads.

“The number and size of these furnaces, together with the use of natural-draft technology, indicate intensive and technologically sophisticated iron production,” says archaeometallurgist Ezekiel Mtetwa of Chicago’s Field Museum of Natural History, who has long studied the furnaces.

Scientists are extremely interested in the peoples who created this and other iron-powered societies across eastern and southern Africa. Research points to an adaptable and innovative group from western Africa as the masterminds. Known as Bantu speakers, these communities began gradually expanding across the continent’s southern half, starting from 6,000 to 4,000 years ago. In doing so, they introduced a fundamentally new, sedentary way of life—characterized by farming, raising livestock, pottery-making, and metalworking—to regions previously dominated by hunter-gatherer communities.

This migration transformed not only the region’s history and technology, but also its languages: Today, around 350 million Africans speak one of roughly 500 distinct Bantu tongues, from southern African Zulu to eastern African Swahili. And at least as many Africans—as well as many African Americans and Afro-Latinos—carry Bantu ancestry in their DNA.

Reconstructing this expansion has been a tough puzzle, especially given the region’s sparse written historical records and limited research funding. But a unique blend of scientists—linguists, archaeologists, and geneticists—have teamed up to unearth clues about the deep origins of the Bantu-speaking people, their path across Africa, and their relationships with other cultures. Although much debate and uncertainty remain, little by little the work is uncovering one of history’s largest and longest human migrations.

“It completely reshaped the demography in sub-Saharan Africa, and we are really interested in trying to understand it,” says population geneticist Sandra Beleza of the United Kingdom’s University of Leicester.

The first evidence of the Bantu expansion came from observations of similarities among Bantu languages. They’re so alike, in fact, that when the Portuguese explorer Vasco da Gama sailed around the southern tip of Africa in 1497, a Portuguese crew member who had spent time in west-central Africa could reportedly communicate with locals in southeastern Africa.

Linguists working in the 19th century started referring to the language family as Bantu, the plural of muntu, meaning “human being” in many Bantu tongues. Later linguists reasoned that the languages stemmed from an ancestral people who migrated across the continent from western Africa, which harbors the greatest diversity of Bantu languages (as would be expected for where the languages originated).

Soon, archaeologists found supporting evidence: across eastern and southern Africa, pieces of pottery of similar yet diverse styles, alongside first-time traces of village life, metal-making, domesticated animals, and western African crops—all developed within the last 2,000 years. Northern sites usually dated back to earlier time periods than sites farther south. “You can clearly see a north-to-south progression,” says archaeological scientist Shadreck Chirikure of the University of Oxford.

By the 1990s, however, some scholars began to question the idea that Bantu languages and skills spread through a migration. Couldn’t these cultural habits have spread while people stayed in the same place, with hunter gatherer communities passing along new technologies, languages, and habits to each other?

Genetic techniques that could analyze people’s ancestry helped to resolve that debate. All of us share DNA with our relatives: 50 percent on average with siblings, 12.5 percent with cousins, 3.3 percent with second cousins, and so on. Though these proportions become minute the further back in time a shared common ancestor lived, statistical techniques can tease out patterns.

Sure enough, a 2009 genetic analysis of 2,432 Africans found that Bantu-speaking people from western, central, and southern Africa are much more closely related than one might expect given their geographic distance. And in 2024, a team of scientists analyzed the genomes of 1,763 Africans and found the greatest genetic diversity among Bantu speakers in western Africa. As distance from the region increased, diversity declined. “This is a typical signature of a population expanding from one place to another,” explains coauthor Carina Schlebusch, a geneticist at Uppsala University in Sweden.

But while linguistic and genetic data point to western Africa—particularly the Grassfields region of Cameroon—as the Bantu homeland, conclusive archaeological proof is thin. At the best-studied archaeological site in the Grassfields, a rock shelter called Shum Laka, pottery fragments appear dating from 7,000 to 6,000 years ago and become abundant by 5,000 to 4,000 years ago. Bowls and jars at first decorated simply with incisions and stamps and later bearing the marks of carved or braided tools rolled over wet clay are a possible, yet inconclusive, trace of Bantu ancestors.

South of Cameroon, archaeologists have searched for artifacts in one of the Bantu-speaking people’s first destinations: the Congo rainforest. A handful of times between 2010 and 2023, archaeologist Alexandre Livingstone Smith of Belgium’s Royal Museum for Central Africa traveled via boat along the Congo River as part of a team led by linguist Birgit Ricquier. In a number of villages, Smith found pottery shards of a style consistent with that found farther west, dating back to 2,300 to 2,000 years ago. “That would be the potential first Bantu settlers of the forest,” Smith says.

Many experts interpret the trails of pottery as evidence that ceramics traditions were brought by Bantu-speaking people all the way from their original home. What’s unclear, however, is when and how Bantu-speaking peoples acquired their farming and metalworking skills—and, in particular, whether they invented iron smelting themselves or learned it from neighboring, iron-extracting cultures.

Scientists have been able to deduce the path that Bantu speakers took to eastern and southern Africa. Some scholars have argued that the path split into two before entering the Congo rainforest, with one group heading east while another went south through the forest. But evidence is now mounting that these groups crossed the rainforest together, and only upon reaching its southern fringes roughly 2,500 to 3,500 years ago did they split into east-moving and southward-moving groups.

Some of this evidence comes from linguistic studies. Not unlike the evolution of species, languages diverge and transform over time, creating “trees” that linguists can reconstruct from similarities in grammar and vocabulary. In 2015, a group of researchers did this computationally using data from about 400 Bantu languages. They observed a much larger diversity—and deeper splits—among the Bantu languages spoken in the Congo rainforest, suggesting that Bantu-speaking ancestors spent a long time there, allowing their languages to diversify. Languages spoken farther east and farther south appeared to stem from a less diverse subset of this larger group, explains coauthor Koen Bostoen, a linguist at Belgium’s Ghent University.

Later linguistic studies, as well as genetic analyses, have reached similar conclusions. In 2023, Beleza and her colleagues compared the genomes of Bantu speakers from Angola—south of the Congo rainforest—with those from the eastern African country of Mozambique and ones from Cameroon, the Bantu homeland.

Remarkably, the eastern African samples were more genetically similar to those from Angola, hundreds of miles to the south of Cameroon and south of the rainforest, than to any of the samples from Cameroon. That points to a recent common ancestor in the Angola region—suggesting that the Bantu split into eastern and southern streams only once they reached the rainforest’s southern edge, Beleza explains.

By the time the Bantu people commenced their movement east and south, they had already come into their own as a farming, iron-making, livestock-herding, and pottery-making people, Chirikure says: Many ancient Bantu settlements across eastern and southern Africa contain evidence of this Bantu “package.”

Key to the Bantu’s success in southern and eastern Africa, experts suspect, was their sedentary lifestyle. This—and their iron tools—would have allowed them to produce more food and grow in number, propelling them to occupy new territories. Their lifestyle affected the landscape as forests turned into farmland, and it also affected the hunter-gatherer communities who were already living there. Some groups were likely crowded out and disappeared, experts suspect, while others were probably pushed to marginal regions, including Indigenous groups such as rainforest hunter-gatherers and Khoisan peoples, who continue to live in central and southern Africa, respectively, to this day.

Still, scientists are learning that Bantu peoples had deep relationships with these two groups. In the 2024 genetic analysis, Schlebusch and her colleagues found that some Bantu-speaking groups in central Africa share about an eighth of their DNA with rainforest hunter-gatherers, and some southern African Bantu speakers share a similar amount with Khoisan groups. Clearly, their ancestors once intermixed.

Curiously, in the case of the Khoisan, this mixing tended to arise from pairings of Bantu-speaking men with Khoisan women. One 2021 study of southern African Bantu speakers found that the DNA inside their mitochondria—which people inherit only from their mothers—had greater-than-expected proportions of Khoisan DNA. But the Y chromosomes, which come from fathers, had much higher proportions of DNA with Bantu ancestry. Some experts speculate that this pattern could be the result of Bantu men raiding Khoisan women or preferentially marrying Khoisan women for whom the traditional bride prices would have been cheaper. “Whenever there is a power difference… between two groups, it tends to melt down to this,” says coauthor Ananyo Choudhury, a geneticist at the University of Witwatersrand in South Africa.

This pattern also left marks on the language. The characteristic clicks of Khoisan languages also occur in southern African Bantu languages Zulu and Khosa. Bostoen suspects that the children of Khoisan-Bantu pairings were, at first, bilingual. Eventually, “they lost their mothers’ languages, but they left a substrate of the mothers’ languages in the Bantu language.”

Archaeologists are also studying the relationships between Bantu farmers and their neighbors. While there may have been conflicts over land, the groups also cooperated with and learned from one another, says Décio Muianga, an archaeologist at Eduardo Mondlane University and the research institute Kaleidoscopio in Maputo, Mozambique. He’s been excavating a rock shelter in Mozambique containing thousands of years’ worth of stone tools, wild animal bones, and wild plant remains—telltale signs of hunter-gatherers living at the site. But interestingly, Bantu-style pottery also appears. “My assumption is that the hunter-gatherers were getting these ceramics from the farmers,” Muianga says. “They were interacting, communicating, and exchanging resources.”

There is evidence, too, for cultural exchanges: European anthropologists have reported that some Bantu-speaking groups would solicit San hunter-gatherers to conduct traditional rainmaking ceremonies, for example.

Bantu social networks eventually extended well beyond Africa. By 1100 to 1600 AD, many of the complex state societies that Bantu speakers built developed trade networks across the Indian Ocean. Archaeological data shows they imported Chinese porcelain and glass beads; in return, they exported gold, ivory, and iron. In fact, a 12th century Muslim cartographer once wrote that iron from parts of southern Africa—likely referring to Great Zimbabwe’s iron-producing hinterlands, Mtetwa suspects—was deemed to be of much higher quality than that from other places like India.

Mtetwa has found evidence that iron smelters near Great Zimbabwe would add bone fragments to the furnaces, perhaps as part of a smelting-blessing ritual, but one with scientific merit: This material contains calcium, which can accelerate the separation of iron from the rest of the ore. That may be a testament to the Great Zimbabweans’ iron-smelting skills: “Some materials introduced within ritual or medicinal practices may simultaneously have had technically beneficial effects on the smelt,” says Mtetwa.

Though they’ve made great progress in reconstructing the Bantu world, scientists are aware of, and trying to address, the limitations of their research so far.

Linguistic studies, for instance, have been a popular tool to reconstruct Bantu history, but Bostoen cautions that very few Bantu languages have been documented thoroughly enough to definitively trace linguistic trees back in time. Linguists may never get a complete dataset, because some old branches of the Bantu language family may have died out.

Another limitation of computational Bantu language-family reconstructions is that they’ve usually assumed that languages have evolved in isolation after different Bantu groups separated. But there may have been extensive cross-pollination among languages as different groups met and interacted.

In fact, when linguists factored in the possibility of contact among Bantu languages in a 2026 study, they couldn’t reconstruct the diversification patterns described by previous linguists, says coauthor Igor Yanovich of the University of Vienna, who wrote about the challenges of Bantu linguistic studies in the 2025 Annual Review of Linguistics. In his view, linguists should first focus on thoroughly documenting Bantu languages before attempting to use them to understand Bantu history.

In hopes of adding new insights, Schlebusch is working to obtain DNA from ancient human remains. By revealing the identity of people who were living in particular places at particular times, this could help to elucidate when and where different Bantu communities moved into, overlapped with, or repopulated areas. “What we see more and more in genetic data is that the movement was probably complex—that it was these ‘spread over spread’ events,” Schlebusch says.

Archaeologists, for their part, hope to fill in the vast gaps in the archaeological record of sub-Saharan Africa—by both conducting new excavations and re-examining existing artifact collections for fresh insights. But all this work requires funding for research and laboratories, especially from African governments, says Chirikure; he, like many of his colleagues, has largely relied on funding from Europe, which is limited. Without more funding, “you are not generating fresh ideas, you are not formulating new hypotheses and testing them using some of the cutting-edge science,” he says.

Scientists from all three disciplines are hopeful that they will eventually tell the complete story of how an innovative people spread and diversified across sub-Saharan Africa, mixing traditions with those of earlier peoples and of others who still live there, to create myriad cultures, each one a unique blend of food, philosophy, language, music, and traditions. “If you travel from West Africa today all the way to East Africa, you have a unique cultural diversity that is very hard to find in many other parts of the world,” Muianga says. Combined with the legacy of other groups, he says, “this is the result of the Bantu migration.”

Anthropic reportedly must face claims its Claude Max ads misled users

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

A federal judge in California found the “5x or 20x more usage” promise plausibly misleading, Courthouse News reported.

A US judge has ruled that Anthropic must face key claims that it misled customers about how much use they get from its Claude Max plans. Magistrate Judge Alex Tse dismissed several other claims on Friday, Courthouse News reported.

The proposed class action, Kahn v. Anthropic, is in the federal court for northern California. TNW has not read the ruling.

Anthropic told buyers that its $100 and $200 Max plans give five or 20 times more usage than its Pro plan. However, the lawsuit, filed in June, says the plans give far less over a full week.

The plaintiffs, Karl Kahn and Solomon Smith, calculated that for one Claude model, the $200 plan gives six times Pro’s weekly usage. The $100 plan gives 3.5 times, they said.

The gap comes from how limits work. Max users get five or 20 times more use in each five-hour session, but their weekly limits are not five or 20 times higher.

Anthropic argued that customers should understand the extra usage as applying per session, and pointed to a help page. Tse was not persuaded, according to Courthouse News.

“Anthropic’s per session disclosure is located two hyperlinks away from the primary sales pages on claude.com,” Tse wrote.

Because customers pay monthly, they could reasonably read the promise as applying each month, the judge found. The plaintiffs can go ahead with claims of negligent misrepresentation and breach of contract.

Meanwhile, Tse dismissed claims under two California laws on unfair business practices and false advertising. He also dismissed Kahn’s claim under a state consumer law, but gave him the chance to add detail. Smith’s claim under that law survived.

Tse also rejected a claim that Anthropic acted unfairly by tightening usage limits during billing periods. Anthropic and the plaintiffs did not immediately respond to Courthouse News. Separately, Anthropic renamed the Max plans last week.

Calling Washington Backpackers: This $5,000 Grant Could Fund Your PCT Thru-Hike

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Walking may be free, but a thru-hike is decidedly expensive: Some Pacific Crest Trail trekkers run up a bill north of $10,000 during a months-long hike. That cost, plus the need to take off work for half a year, presents massive barriers to many would-be thru-hikers.

Seattleite Geoff Burg knows this—and he wants to help. With fellow Washington hikers Keya Gillenwater and Madelyn Scherrer, Burg launched the Pacific Crest Trail Grant in 2025 to award $5,000 to a Washington-based hiker with dreams of completing the trail.

Burg’s connection to the PCT goes back a decade. The attorney was hiking with his kids near Stevens Pass in 2016 when he came across some hikers in distress. Burg volunteered to drive the duo, a pair of PCT thru-hikers in their 60s, into town for medical supplies, then offered them a place to stay in his family’s home. The couple told Burg all about their thru-hike and ultralight philosophy—and the encounter stuck with him.

Over the next several years, Burg chipped away at a section hike of the Washington segment of the PCT. He fell in love with being on the trail, alone or with his kids, and with the greater hiking community around it. Several years later, a conversation with his massage therapist sparked the idea of a scholarship. Well established in his career, and with experience as an advisor for a youth outdoor leadership program, Burg was in a position to help others achieve their long trail dreams.

“We give scholarships for football and baseball and everything else,” he says. “Why not for hiking?”

So he connected with former mentees Gillenwater and Scherrer, a PCT alum, and formed a committee. The grant would offer financial support to a young Washingtonian hoping to complete the long trail.

Burg especially wanted to support young hikers with a financial need; applicants must be between the ages of 18 and 33, with strong ties to Washington state. They should also be able to demonstrate past backpacking experience, which Burg hopes will improve the recipient’s chances of succeeding on a thru-hike and paying the experience forward through volunteer projects like youth outreach or LNT education. Grant recipients who meet that criteria are chosen via a lottery, and must stay in touch with the grant committee during their hikes. Burg emphasizes that the process is not merit-based.

“We already have a community of people that can do this or who want to do this, but within that broader community, there’s so many people that can’t afford it,” he says.

Thirty-two-year-old Seattleite Kayla Provost was the grant’s first recipient. She completed her trek in September 2026, and says she couldn’t have finished the thru-hike without the financial support.

Provost had previously worked as a naturalist guide and, as a Washington resident, felt deeply connected to the Cascades. After losing her mom a few years prior, Provost felt compelled to change her lifestyle. She felt called to the PCT, but financial hurdles posed challenges. After searching “grants for thru-hiking” online, Provost stumbled across a Reddit post about the PCT Grant.

“The grant was truly a lifeline for me,” she says. “Beyond the financial support, the monthly check-ins with the grant committee gave me a sense of accountability and reminded me I had people cheering me on.”

She says the experience redoubled her desire to facilitate outdoor experiences for other people.

“It is incredibly powerful to make a choice, trust yourself enough to follow through, and learn to be comfortable with the uncomfortable,” she says. “The trail offered me a deep sense of confidence, gratitude, and trust in my own ability to navigate the unknown.”

The grant will fund its second thru-hiker in 2027. Through donations, Burg hopes to grow the program into a self-sustaining fund to support more hikers on more trails.

Applications for prospective 2027 PCT thru-hikers are currently open. Washingtonians who meet the criteria can apply by January 31.
